Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.

When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.

Like that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.

industrial-history talks I used to enjoy because the old machinery pictures set me off.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the shift and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s a whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I don’t trust that plant or the people running it anymore, and some days I feel like I’m damaged goods who can’t even do the job I spent my life getting good at.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks or stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I keep snapping at people, like at that safety meeting when I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed so many shifts and lost so much patience with trainees that I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that machinery accident after the overnight schedule change, about 2 years and 4 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Even if you didn’t snap or act on it, have you felt yourself getting Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.

I just started yelling before I could stop myself, and afterward I felt awful.\n\nOutside of work, I get irritated, sure, but the real snapping—where people actually pull back from me—that’s been more there. Now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me a question, and that bothers me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week, outside of work—like at home, in a store, or in traffic—when you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry, even if you didn’t act on it?\n\nPatient: In the last week… outside work, I don’t know, I get kind of wound up, but the thing that really sticks is still work. I know you’re asking home or traffic or a store, but I keep seeing that safety meeting in my head, where I just started yelling before I even had a chance to think. \n\nAnd it wasn’t like me, or I don’t want it to be like me. Now those younger inspectors kind of hover or avoid asking me things, like they’re scared I’m going to snap at them. That’s what makes me feel like something’s really off. Outside of work, I can feel that irritation sitting there, but I can’t think of one clear moment this week the way I can with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you didn’t snap or act on it, have you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry in any situation outside of work—like at home, in a store, or while driving—over the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve felt it… I mean, not like I threw something in a store or anything. It’s more this heat that comes up fast, and then I’m trying to swallow it down.\n\nBut honestly, the clearest thing is still why I’m here—because I keep snapping at people. Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I guess I still don’t really know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?

I know you’re asking home or traffic or a store, but I keep seeing that safety meeting in my head, where I just started yelling before I even had a chance to think. \n\nAnd it wasn’t like me, or I don’t want it to be like me. Now those younger inspectors kind of hover or avoid asking me things, like they’re scared I’m going to snap at them. That’s what makes me feel like something’s really off. Outside of work, I can feel that irritation sitting there, but I can’t think of one clear moment this week the way I can with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you didn’t snap or act on it, have you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry in any situation outside of work—like at home, in a store, or while driving—over the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve felt it… I mean, not like I threw something in a store or anything. It’s more this heat that comes up fast, and then I’m trying to swallow it down.\n\nBut honestly, the clearest thing is still why I’m here—because I keep snapping at people. Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.\n\nBut the reason I’m here is really because it’s coming out at people. Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.\n\nLike that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nPatient: At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.\n\nThe thing that really scared me was that safety meeting. I just started yelling before I even felt like I’d decided to, and then afterward I’m looking at these younger inspectors and they’re acting like they’re afraid to ask me a simple question. That sticks with me. So even when I’m in my own kitchen or whatever, it’s like I’m still back there, getting irritated all over again thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that sudden rush of anger or irritability, do you notice any physical reactions in your body, like your heart racing, muscles tightening, or feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice I’m keyed up, but what stands out more is that I snap. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. I don’t want to be that person, but it keeps coming out at people.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific moments in the past week outside of work—like at home, in public, or during personal interactions—when you felt a strong surge of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it?\n\nPatient: This past week… outside of work, I don’t know, I keep trying to separate it, but it all kind of follows me home. I’ll be at home doing some stupid thing, like making coffee or picking up around the kitchen, and then I think about that safety meeting again.\n\nI know I already mentioned it, but that’s the thing that keeps coming up. I started yelling before I could even stop myself, and now those younger inspectors look at me like they’re scared to ask me anything. So even when I’m not at work, I get that surge, like I’m irritated all over again—and then mad at myself for being that way. I don’t always say anything out loud at home, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it like being “down,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things you normally do at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?\n\nLately I’ll start something and then wander off, or the TV’s on and I couldn’t tell you what happened in the episode. But then again, I’ve always been like that some evenings after work. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you stopped doing things you used to care about or find enjoyable, not just putting them off but not really wanting to do them anymore?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can say I’ve stopped caring. That sounds so final, you know? Like throwing something in the trash and saying, well, that’s done.

I mean, I still think about doing things. I’ll see something in the yard that needs attention, or I’ll remember I was going to fix something, and then my mind goes off in ten directions. My garage is a mess right now too—there’s a box of old screws I’ve been meaning to sort, and then I find a receipt from years ago and start wondering why I even kept it.

So… not really “I don’t want to anymore,” I guess. More like I don’t get to it, or I can’t settle into it. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.

put me right back in that bay, seeing my coworker by the machine like it’s happening in front of me all over again.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay, I won’t talk about the accident if I can help it, and I quit going to the industrial-history talks I used to enjoy because the old machinery pictures set me off.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the shift and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s a whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I don’t trust that plant or the people running it anymore, and some days I feel like I’m damaged goods who can’t even do the job I spent my life getting good at.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks or stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I keep snapping at people, like at that safety meeting when I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Even in the break room or at rehearsal, I’m watching doors, listening for sudden machine noises, and checking who’s moving behind me because my body won’t believe I’m safe.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed so many shifts and lost so much patience with trainees that I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that machinery accident after the overnight schedule change, about 2 years and 4 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it like being “down,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things you normally do at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know. But then again, I’ve always been like that some evenings after work. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to care about or find enjoyable, not just putting them off but not really wanting to do them anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve stopped caring. That sounds so final, you know? Like throwing something in the trash and saying, well, that’s done.\n\nI mean, I still think about doing things. I’ll see something in the yard that needs attention, or I’ll remember I was going to fix something, and then my mind goes off in ten directions. My garage is a mess right now too—there’s a box of old screws I’ve been meaning to sort, and then I find a receipt from years ago and start wondering why I even kept it.\n\nSo… not really “I don’t want to anymore,” I guess. More like I don’t get to it, or I can’t settle into it. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even things that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.

I keep going back over it in my head, like if I’d pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right… maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. And I know people say you can’t know that, there were other people there, procedures and all that, but it’s hard not to think, well, I was there too. I noticed something felt off.

It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you locked the door, except it’s not something small like that. It just loops around. I’ll be doing dishes or looking for something in a drawer, and there it is again—why didn’t I say it louder, why didn’t I make them listen.

They act like I’m going to bite their heads off.\n\nAt home or out somewhere… I can feel that same edge sometimes, like little things hit me harder than they should, but the big snapping, the kind that makes me feel ashamed after, that’s mostly been happening around people at work.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time outside of work when you found yourself getting angry or snapping at someone, even over something small?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one outside work… and I keep coming back to work, honestly. That safety meeting, I know I already mentioned it, but it’s like the main one stuck in my head. I just started yelling before I could stop myself, and afterward I felt awful.\n\nOutside of work, I get irritated, sure, but the real snapping—where people actually pull back from me—that’s been more there. Now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me a question, and that bothers me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week, outside of work—like at home, in a store, or in traffic—when you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry, even if you didn’t act on it?\n\nPatient: In the last week… outside work, I don’t know, I get kind of wound up, but the thing that really sticks is still work. I know you’re asking home or traffic or a store, but I keep seeing that safety meeting in my head, where I just started yelling before I even had a chance to think. \n\nAnd it wasn’t like me, or I don’t want it to be like me. Now those younger inspectors kind of hover or avoid asking me things, like they’re scared I’m going to snap at them. That’s what makes me feel like something’s really off. Outside of work, I can feel that irritation sitting there, but I can’t think of one clear moment this week the way I can with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you didn’t snap or act on it, have you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry in any situation outside of work—like at home, in a store, or while driving—over the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve felt it… I mean, not like I threw something in a store or anything. It’s more this heat that comes up fast, and then I’m trying to swallow it down.\n\nBut honestly, the clearest thing is still why I’m here—because I keep snapping at people. Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.\n\nBut the reason I’m here is really because it’s coming out at people. Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.\n\nLike that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nPatient: At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.\n\nThe thing that really scared me was that safety meeting. I just started yelling before I even felt like I’d decided to, and then afterward I’m looking at these younger inspectors and they’re acting like they’re afraid to ask me a simple question. That sticks with me. So even when I’m in my own kitchen or whatever, it’s like I’m still back there, getting irritated all over again thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that sudden rush of anger or irritability, do you notice any physical reactions in your body, like your heart racing, muscles tightening, or feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice I’m keyed up, but what stands out more is that I snap. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they act like they’re scared to ask me anything, like they’re waiting for me to blow up. That’s why I’m here. I don’t want to be that person, but it keeps coming out at people.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific moments in the past week outside of work—like at home, in public, or during personal interactions—when you felt a strong surge of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it?\n\nPatient: This past week… outside of work, I don’t know, I keep trying to separate it, but it all kind of follows me home. I’ll be at home doing some stupid thing, like making coffee or picking up around the kitchen, and then I think about that safety meeting again.\n\nI know I already mentioned it, but that’s the thing that keeps coming up. I started yelling before I could even stop myself, and now those younger inspectors look at me like they’re scared to ask me anything. So even when I’m not at work, I get that surge, like I’m irritated all over again—and then mad at myself for being that way. I don’t always say anything out loud at home, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it like being “down,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things you normally do at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?\n\nLately I’ll start something and then wander off, or the TV’s on and I couldn’t tell you what happened in the episode. But then again, I’ve always been like that some evenings after work. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to care about or find enjoyable, not just putting them off but not really wanting to do them anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve stopped caring. I’ll see something in the yard that needs attention, or I’ll remember I was going to fix something, and then my mind goes off in ten directions. My garage is a mess right now too—there’s a box of old screws I’ve been meaning to sort, and then I find a receipt from years ago and start wondering why I even kept it.\n\nSo… not really “I don’t want to anymore,” I guess. More like I don’t get to it, or I can’t settle into it. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even things that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. I noticed something felt off.\n\nIt’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you locked the door, except it’s not something small like that. It just loops around. I’m not sure I’d say “down,” exactly. I mean, I have days where I just sort of sit there and don’t get much done, but then everybody has days like that, right?

At home, I still think about the things I usually do. Like I’ll walk past the laundry room and notice the shelf needs fixing, and then I’ll remember I was supposed to call about something else, and then I end up looking for a screwdriver that somehow isn’t where it’s supposed to be. My husband used to say I had three projects going before breakfast, and… well, I guess that’s still true, except now they don’t really get finished.

But whether that’s losing interest or just being scattered, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t care about this anymore.” It’s more like the day gets away from me.

Outside of work, I can feel that irritation sitting there, but I can’t think of one clear moment this week the way I can with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you didn’t snap or act on it, have you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry in any situation outside of work—like at home, in a store, or while driving—over the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve felt it… I mean, not like I threw something in a store or anything. It’s more this heat that comes up fast, and then I’m trying to swallow it down.\n\nBut honestly, the clearest thing is still why I’m here—because I keep snapping at people. Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.\n\nBut the reason I’m here is really because it’s coming out at people. Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.\n\nLike that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nPatient: At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.\n\nThe thing that really scared me was that safety meeting. I just started yelling before I even felt like I’d decided to, and then afterward I’m looking at these younger inspectors and they’re acting like they’re afraid to ask me a simple question. That sticks with me. So even when I’m in my own kitchen or whatever, it’s like I’m still back there, getting irritated all over again thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that sudden rush of anger or irritability, do you notice any physical reactions in your body, like your heart racing, muscles tightening, or feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice I’m keyed up, but what stands out more is that I snap. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they act like they’re scared to ask me anything, like they’re waiting for me to blow up. That’s why I’m here. I don’t want to be that person, but it keeps coming out at people.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific moments in the past week outside of work—like at home, in public, or during personal interactions—when you felt a strong surge of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it?\n\nPatient: This past week… outside of work, I don’t know, I keep trying to separate it, but it all kind of follows me home. I’ll be at home doing some stupid thing, like making coffee or picking up around the kitchen, and then I think about that safety meeting again.\n\nI know I already mentioned it, but that’s the thing that keeps coming up. I started yelling before I could even stop myself, and now those younger inspectors look at me like they’re scared to ask me anything. So even when I’m not at work, I get that surge, like I’m irritated all over again—and then mad at myself for being that way. I don’t always say anything out loud at home, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it like being “down,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things you normally do at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?\n\nLately I’ll start something and then wander off, or the TV’s on and I couldn’t tell you what happened in the episode. But then again, I’ve always been like that some evenings after work. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to care about or find enjoyable, not just putting them off but not really wanting to do them anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve stopped caring. That sounds so final, you know? Like throwing something in the trash and saying, well, that’s done.\n\nI mean, I still think about doing things. I’ll see something in the yard that needs attention, or I’ll remember I was going to fix something, and then my mind goes off in ten directions. My garage is a mess right now too—there’s a box of old screws I’ve been meaning to sort, and then I find a receipt from years ago and start wondering why I even kept it.\n\nSo… not really “I don’t want to anymore,” I guess. More like I don’t get to it, or I can’t settle into it. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even things that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, like if I’d pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right… maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. And I know people say you can’t know that, there were other people there, procedures and all that, but it’s hard not to think, well, I was there too. I noticed something felt off.\n\nIt’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you locked the door, except it’s not something small like that. It just loops around. I’ll be doing dishes or looking for something in a drawer, and there it is again—why didn’t I say it louder, why didn’t I make them listen.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you felt down or lost interest in things you used to care about, even things you normally do at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “down,” exactly. Like I’ll walk past the laundry room and notice the shelf needs fixing, and then I’ll remember I was supposed to call about something else, and then I end up looking for a screwdriver that somehow isn’t where it’s supposed to be. My husband used to say I had three projects going before breakfast, and… well, I guess that’s still true, except now they don’t really get finished.\n\nBut whether that’s losing interest or just being scattered, I don’t know. I’ll be doing dishes or looking for something in a drawer, and there it is again—why didn’t I say it louder, why didn’t I make them listen.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you felt down or lost interest in things you used to care about, even things you normally do at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “down,” exactly. I mean, I have days where I just sort of sit there and don’t get much done, but then everybody has days like that, right?\n\nAt home, I still think about the things I usually do. Like I’ll walk past the laundry room and notice the shelf needs fixing, and then I’ll remember I was supposed to call about something else, and then I end up looking for a screwdriver that somehow isn’t where it’s supposed to be. My husband used to say I had three projects going before breakfast, and… well, I guess that’s still true, except now they don’t really get finished.\n\nBut whether that’s losing interest or just being scattered, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t care about this anymore.” It’s more like the day gets away from me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt down or had trouble enjoying things, even when you’re not thinking about work or the safety meeting?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. When you put it that way, “even when I’m not thinking about it,” I’m not sure how to separate that out.

I’ll be at home and think I’m just watching TV or folding towels, and then I realize I haven’t really followed what’s on, but that could just be me getting distracted. I’ve always been the kind of person who notices something else that needs doing—like the cabinet handle being loose, or the junk drawer getting out of hand—and then suddenly I’m standing there with a rubber band and three old batteries wondering what I came in for.

So… trouble enjoying things? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s because I’m not enjoying them or because my mind just won’t stay put long enough. I wouldn’t want to say it’s one thing if it’s not.

It’s more this heat that comes up fast, and then I’m trying to swallow it down.\n\nBut honestly, the clearest thing is still why I’m here—because I keep snapping at people. Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.\n\nBut the reason I’m here is really because it’s coming out at people. Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.\n\nLike that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nPatient: At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.\n\nThe thing that really scared me was that safety meeting. I just started yelling before I even felt like I’d decided to, and then afterward I’m looking at these younger inspectors and they’re acting like they’re afraid to ask me a simple question. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they act like they’re scared to ask me anything, like they’re waiting for me to blow up. That’s why I’m here. I don’t want to be that person, but it keeps coming out at people.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific moments in the past week outside of work—like at home, in public, or during personal interactions—when you felt a strong surge of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it?\n\nPatient: This past week… outside of work, I don’t know, I keep trying to separate it, but it all kind of follows me home. I’ll be at home doing some stupid thing, like making coffee or picking up around the kitchen, and then I think about that safety meeting again.\n\nI know I already mentioned it, but that’s the thing that keeps coming up. I started yelling before I could even stop myself, and now those younger inspectors look at me like they’re scared to ask me anything. So even when I’m not at work, I get that surge, like I’m irritated all over again—and then mad at myself for being that way. I don’t always say anything out loud at home, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?\n\nLately I’ll start something and then wander off, or the TV’s on and I couldn’t tell you what happened in the episode. I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.\n\nBut the reason I’m here is really because it’s coming out at people. Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.\n\nLike that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nPatient: At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.\n\nThe thing that really scared me was that safety meeting. I just started yelling before I even felt like I’d decided to, and then afterward I’m looking at these younger inspectors and they’re acting like they’re afraid to ask me a simple question. That sticks with me. So even when I’m in my own kitchen or whatever, it’s like I’m still back there, getting irritated all over again thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that sudden rush of anger or irritability, do you notice any physical reactions in your body, like your heart racing, muscles tightening, or feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice I’m keyed up, but what stands out more is that I snap. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they act like they’re scared to ask me anything, like they’re waiting for me to blow up. That’s why I’m here. I don’t want to be that person, but it keeps coming out at people.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific moments in the past week outside of work—like at home, in public, or during personal interactions—when you felt a strong surge of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it?\n\nPatient: This past week… outside of work, I don’t know, I keep trying to separate it, but it all kind of follows me home. I’ll be at home doing some stupid thing, like making coffee or picking up around the kitchen, and then I think about that safety meeting again.\n\nI know I already mentioned it, but that’s the thing that keeps coming up. I started yelling before I could even stop myself, and now those younger inspectors look at me like they’re scared to ask me anything. So even when I’m not at work, I get that surge, like I’m irritated all over again—and then mad at myself for being that way. I don’t always say anything out loud at home, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it like being “down,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things you normally do at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?\n\nLately I’ll start something and then wander off, or the TV’s on and I couldn’t tell you what happened in the episode. But then again, I’ve always been like that some evenings after work. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to care about or find enjoyable, not just putting them off but not really wanting to do them anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve stopped caring. That sounds so final, you know? Like throwing something in the trash and saying, well, that’s done.\n\nI mean, I still think about doing things. I’ll see something in the yard that needs attention, or I’ll remember I was going to fix something, and then my mind goes off in ten directions. My garage is a mess right now too—there’s a box of old screws I’ve been meaning to sort, and then I find a receipt from years ago and start wondering why I even kept it.\n\nSo… not really “I don’t want to anymore,” I guess. More like I don’t get to it, or I can’t settle into it. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even things that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, like if I’d pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right… maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. And I know people say you can’t know that, there were other people there, procedures and all that, but it’s hard not to think, well, I was there too. I noticed something felt off.\n\nIt’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you locked the door, except it’s not something small like that. It just loops around. I’ll be doing dishes or looking for something in a drawer, and there it is again—why didn’t I say it louder, why didn’t I make them listen.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you felt down or lost interest in things you used to care about, even things you normally do at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “down,” exactly. I mean, I have days where I just sort of sit there and don’t get much done, but then everybody has days like that, right?\n\nAt home, I still think about the things I usually do. Like I’ll walk past the laundry room and notice the shelf needs fixing, and then I’ll remember I was supposed to call about something else, and then I end up looking for a screwdriver that somehow isn’t where it’s supposed to be. My husband used to say I had three projects going before breakfast, and… well, I guess that’s still true, except now they don’t really get finished.\n\nBut whether that’s losing interest or just being scattered, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t care about this anymore.” It’s more like the day gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or had trouble enjoying things, even when you’re not thinking about work or the safety meeting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Outside of work, I can feel that irritation sitting there, but I can’t think of one clear moment this week the way I can with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you didn’t snap or act on it, have you felt yourself getting noticeably irritated or angry in any situation outside of work—like at home, in a store, or while driving—over the past week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve felt it… I mean, not like I threw something in a store or anything. It’s more this heat that comes up fast, and then I’m trying to swallow it down.\n\nBut honestly, the clearest thing is still why I’m here—because I keep snapping at people. Like that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. That gets in my head. So outside work, yes, I can feel that same irritability there, but the place it really comes out is with people at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling irritated or angry even when you’re alone or not interacting with others, like when you’re at home relaxing or doing something by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I’m alone it’s harder to say, because there’s nobody there to actually catch it, you know? I’ll be sitting there and my mind goes back to things, and I can feel myself getting worked up.\n\nBut the reason I’m here is really because it’s coming out at people. Like that safety meeting—I just started yelling before I could stop myself. And now the younger inspectors, they act scared to ask me anything, like I’m going to blow up on them. That’s the part that bothers me. Even if I’m alone and irritated, it’s what happens around people that makes me think… this isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel irritated or angry when you’re alone—like what sets it off and how long it tends to last?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s kind of… it’ll start with me thinking about something that happened, and then my head goes right back to work. I know you asked about alone, but that’s where it goes.\n\nLike that safety meeting. I keep replaying how I started yelling before I could stop myself, and then I picture those younger inspectors now, how they look nervous around me or don’t want to ask me things. That gets me irritated all over again, and then I’m mad at myself too. I don’t know exactly how long it lasts… sometimes it settles down, but it can stick with me for a while once I get going in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situations at home or in your personal life where you’ve felt a sudden rush of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it outwardly?\n\nPatient: At home… I mean, yeah, I get that rush sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve got a neat example from home. My mind keeps going back to work, honestly.\n\nThe thing that really scared me was that safety meeting. I just started yelling before I even felt like I’d decided to, and then afterward I’m looking at these younger inspectors and they’re acting like they’re afraid to ask me a simple question. That sticks with me. So even when I’m in my own kitchen or whatever, it’s like I’m still back there, getting irritated all over again thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that sudden rush of anger or irritability, do you notice any physical reactions in your body, like your heart racing, muscles tightening, or feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice I’m keyed up, but what stands out more is that I snap. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they act like they’re scared to ask me anything, like they’re waiting for me to blow up. That’s why I’m here. I don’t want to be that person, but it keeps coming out at people.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any specific moments in the past week outside of work—like at home, in public, or during personal interactions—when you felt a strong surge of anger or irritability, even if you didn’t express it?\n\nPatient: This past week… outside of work, I don’t know, I keep trying to separate it, but it all kind of follows me home. I’ll be at home doing some stupid thing, like making coffee or picking up around the kitchen, and then I think about that safety meeting again.\n\nI know I already mentioned it, but that’s the thing that keeps coming up. I started yelling before I could even stop myself, and now those younger inspectors look at me like they’re scared to ask me anything. So even when I’m not at work, I get that surge, like I’m irritated all over again—and then mad at myself for being that way. I don’t always say anything out loud at home, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even things at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I get worn out and I’ll sit there not doing much, but I don’t know if I’d call it “losing interest.” I still do the things I have to do.\n\nFree time is funny anyway—when you work as long as I have, you don’t exactly come home with some big list of hobbies. I’ll put the TV on or mess around the house, but half the time I’m not even paying attention to it. So… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it like being “down,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things you normally do at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know. I mean, I used to do little things around the house, watch a show, maybe putter in the yard if the weather was decent… but I don’t know if I “enjoyed” it or if it was just routine, you know?\n\nLately I’ll start something and then wander off, or the TV’s on and I couldn’t tell you what happened in the episode. But then again, I’ve always been like that some evenings after work. My neighbor keeps asking about this little plant stand I said I’d help her fix, and I keep meaning to, but then I think, well, I need to get this or that done first… and then the day’s gone. So I don’t know if that counts as losing interest or just being tired and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to care about or find enjoyable, not just putting them off but not really wanting to do them anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve stopped caring. That sounds so final, you know? Like throwing something in the trash and saying, well, that’s done.\n\nI mean, I still think about doing things. I’ll see something in the yard that needs attention, or I’ll remember I was going to fix something, and then my mind goes off in ten directions. My garage is a mess right now too—there’s a box of old screws I’ve been meaning to sort, and then I find a receipt from years ago and start wondering why I even kept it.\n\nSo… not really “I don’t want to anymore,” I guess. More like I don’t get to it, or I can’t settle into it. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even things that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, like if I’d pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right… maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. And I know people say you can’t know that, there were other people there, procedures and all that, but it’s hard not to think, well, I was there too. I noticed something felt off.\n\nIt’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you locked the door, except it’s not something small like that. It just loops around. I’ll be doing dishes or looking for something in a drawer, and there it is again—why didn’t I say it louder, why didn’t I make them listen.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you felt down or lost interest in things you used to care about, even things you normally do at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “down,” exactly. I mean, I have days where I just sort of sit there and don’t get much done, but then everybody has days like that, right?\n\nAt home, I still think about the things I usually do. Like I’ll walk past the laundry room and notice the shelf needs fixing, and then I’ll remember I was supposed to call about something else, and then I end up looking for a screwdriver that somehow isn’t where it’s supposed to be. My husband used to say I had three projects going before breakfast, and… well, I guess that’s still true, except now they don’t really get finished.\n\nBut whether that’s losing interest or just being scattered, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t care about this anymore.” It’s more like the day gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or had trouble enjoying things, even when you’re not thinking about work or the safety meeting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When you put it that way, “even when I’m not thinking about it,” I’m not sure how to separate that out.\n\nI’ll be at home and think I’m just watching TV or folding towels, and then I realize I haven’t really followed what’s on, but that could just be me getting distracted. I’ve always been the kind of person who notices something else that needs doing—like the cabinet handle being loose, or the junk drawer getting out of hand—and then suddenly I’m standing there with a rubber band and three old batteries wondering what I came in for.\n\nSo… trouble enjoying things? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s because I’m not enjoying them or because my mind just won’t stay put long enough. I wouldn’t want to say it’s one thing if it’s not.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific moment in the past week, when you were not at work—like at home, in a store, or while driving—when you felt a clear sense of irritability or anger, even if you didn’t show it outwardly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can. Not really a store or anything, but at home this week my husband asked me something simple—I think where I’d put a receipt or maybe the tape measure, because I’m always moving things and then accusing the house of eating them—and I just snapped. Way sharper than I needed to.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I keep doing that. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I even realized how loud I was getting. And now some of the younger inspectors… they kind of hesitate before they ask me anything, like they’re scared I’m going to bite their head off.\n\nI don’t like that. I’m not trying to be that person. It just comes up so fast.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
